#994. Kerajaan Malaysia Perkenal Inisiatif Awan AI Berdaulat Untuk Mempertegas Selera Data

Laporan ini merangkumi pelancaran inisiatif "Awan AI Berdaulat" oleh Kerajaan Malaysia untuk memper data sensitif negara. Laporan laporan menekankan keyakinan infrastruktur awan ini akan melindungi selera data negara dan menjaga kedaulatan digital,...

Berita mengenai pelancaran inisiatif "Awan AI Berdaulat" oleh Menteri Komunikasi Fahmi Fadzil telah mendominasi heading utama beberapa media tempatan dalam 24 jam lepas. Fokus utama laporan ini menggariskan keyakinan bahawa infrastruktur awan ini akan melindungi selera data negara dan mempertegas kedaulatan digital Malaysia. Namun, sekiranya kita mengelihat di balik narasi pengiktirafan, ada beberapa lapisan kritikal yang belum terlalu terpapar dalam isian media massal.

Yang dilaporkan ialah Fahmi Fadzil menyatakan bahawa projek ini adalah respons terhadap keperluan melindungi maklumat sensitif negara dari pihak luar, terutamanya dalam konteks persaingan teknologi global. Laporan NST Online dan Astro Awani menegaskan keyakinan bahawa data warga Malaysia akan disimpan di dalam negeri, sehingga menghindari eksportasi maklumat yang tidak perlu. Ini adalah langkah yang menjadi sangat relevan setelah beberapa skandal data dan peningkatan tahap pengawasan digital di peringkat antarabangsa.

Namun, di sisi lain, terdapat laporan dari Free Malaysia Today yang membicarakan sudut pandang yang agak skeptik. Menurut Shahril, tidak ada rancangan jelas mengenai manfaat ekonomi AI bagi Malaysia. Ini mencipta kontras yang menarik: satu pihak kerajaan menggariskan impian pengawasan dan perlindungan, manakala pihak lain menanya soalan mendasar mengenai pencapaian ekonomi sebenar. Gap ini menimbulkan pertanyaan tentang sejauh mana inisiatif ini sebenarnya akan menghasilkan nilai tambah bagi rakyat biasa, atau hanya menjadi kos politik sekadar.

Secara kejadian, laporan Bernama juga menunjukkan Malaysia sedang aktif memperkuat kerjasama ASEAN dalam menangani ancaman Siber yang berasaskan AI. Ini menambah lapisan geopolitik di atas sekadar isu teknologi dalam negeri. Kerjasama ini kemungkinan besar bertujuan untuk menyelaralkan standard dan prosedur awam, namun di antara garis-garis ini, siapa sebenarnya yang menguntungkan dari kerangka regulasi yang dibangun? Apakah ini akan menjadi platform bagi perusahaan teknologi tempatan untuk berkembang, atau sekadar menjadi lorong bagi pemain luar negeri untuk memasuki pasar dengan kaedah yang lebih mudah?

Bagi membaca yang mengikut perkembangan AI di Malaysia, terdapat beberapa soalan yang mendasar namun belum terjawab sepenuhnya dalam laporan ini. Pertanyaan pertama adalah mengenai wang yang dialokasikan untuk membangun infrastruktur ini — adakah wang pemborong itu diterbitkan dengan transparan dan betapa besar tanggungjawab yang dibawa oleh syarikat-syarikat awan antarabangsa yang terlibat? Soalan kedua menyangkit aspek privasi: meskipun data disimpan dalam negeri, tahap pengiktirafan dan enkripsi digunakan sama ada memenuhi piawaian data yang ketat atau hanya sekadar kerangka susun lapis teknologi biasa?

Secara keseluruhan, narasi rasmi menonjolkan impian pengawasan digital, namun realiti di lapangan menunjukkan perselisihan antara impian pengawasan dengan tanggungjawab ekonomi dan operasi. Inisiatif Awan AI Berdaulat memang membawa perubahan paradigma cara negara berurusan dengan data, tetapi sejauh mana ia akan merangsang ekonomi domestik dan melindungi hak asasi digital rakyat tetap menjadi perbincangan yang membutuhkan bukti konkret, bukan sekadar janji politik.

Laporan FMT menegaskan bahawa masih terdapat kekosongan dalam aspek manfaat ekonomi AI yang jelas. Mengapa kerajaan masih melancarkan projek besar sekalipun belum ada data kuantitatif yang menunjukkan pertumbuhan PDB atau ciptaan kerja langsung yang boleh diait kepada penggunaan AI? Ini menimbulkan persepsi bahawa inisiatif ini mungkin lebih fokus pada aspek pengawasan dan kemampuan teknologi daripada memberi hasil sektor yang konkret bagi rakyat Malaysia.

Untuk memahami kekosongan ini, perlu kita mengkaji bagaimana negara-negara maju lain telah mengintegrasikan AI ke dalam struktur ekonomi mereka. Di negara seperti Singapura, AI tidak lagi dianggap sekadar alat perisian, tetapi sebagai infrastruktur strategis yang memutarbalik sektor perbankan, logistik, dan perubatan. Keberhasilan Singapura didorong oleh perpaduan antara dana kerajaan untuk penyelidikan dan perkembangan (R&D) serta pelan privat yang telus. Malaysia, dengan demikian, menghadapi tantangan unik. Kita memiliki kumpulan tenaga ahli yang hebat, tetapi ekosistem startup AI yang masih berusia kecil dan bergantung tinggi kepada jaringan global.

Secara sejarah, inisiatif teknologi besar seperti pembangunan National Data Grid atau pembangunan infrastruktur 4G awal memerlukan waktu bertahun-tahun sebelum manfaat ekonomi yang measurable terlihat. Dengan AI, soalan serupa muncul. Adakah wang yang dialokasikan untuk Awan AI Berdaulat akan digunakan untuk membekalkan subsidi kepada pelabur asing untuk membina pusat data, atau untuk membangun kapasiti dalam negeri? Tanpa kerangka ganjaran yang jelas atau cukai teknologi yang merangsang, ada risiko bahawa proyek ini akan menjadi "white elephant" — mahal dibangun tetapi digunakan tidak sepenuhnya atau hanya sekadar menampung data yang jarang diakses. Perspektif ekonomi AI juga melibatkan aspek produktiviti. AI boleh membantu mengautomatikkan tugas-tugas repetitif di kalangan pekerja awam dan swasta, tetapi perlaksanaan ini memerlukan latihan ketelusan dan ubah budaya organisasi. Jika projek ini hanya berhenti di lapisan infrastruktur tanpa menyentuh lapisan aplikasi dan jaringan manusia, maka persoalan Shahril tentang "tanpa manfaat ekonomi jelas" akan menjadi kenyataan yang mengesakan.

Kerjasama ASEAN: Siapa yang Mendapati Keuntungan?

Laporan Bernama menyebutkan upaya memperkuat kerjasama ASEAN terhadap ancaman Siber berasaskan AI. Di sinilah kita melihat kerangka geopolitik yang tersembunyi. Negara-negara anggota ASEAN mempunyai tahap penguasaan teknologi dan infrastruktur yang berbeza. Malaysia, sebagai pemain yang aktif, kemungkinan besar akan menjadi jembatan, tetapi pertanyaan yang perlu diajukan adalah sama ada kerangka ini akan meringkaskan kuasa kepada pemain besar atau justru mencipta peluang bagi startup teknologi lokal untuk bersaing di tahap antarabangsa.

Perspektif ASEAN tentang keamanan Siber pernah lama dikendalikan oleh konsep "Centrality" — di mana ASEAN berusaha menjadi pusat dialog antara besar-besaran. Dalam konteks AI dan Siber, dinamika ini menjadi lebih kompleks. Malaysia, melalui Awan AI Berdaulat, menempatkan diri sebagai pemain yang menuntut data berada di dalam negeri. Ini boleh menjadi nilai menambah jika kerjasama ASEAN membolehkan pertukaran data yang selamat tanpa mengorbankan privasi individu. Namun, di sisi lain, ada risiko "data colonialisme". Negara-negara dengan infrastruktur awan yang lebih matang, seperti Singapura atau Thailand, mungkin membawa teknologi dan kaedah analisis yang melekat, sementara data Malaysia hanya digunakan sebagai bahan baku untuk model-model mereka.

Apakah kerjasama ini akan menguntungkan mikro dan kecil usaha (SME) teknologi Malaysia? Jika kerangka regulasi ASEAN boleh menyelaraskan standard pengiktirafan data (data governance), maka pelabur asing akan lebih mudah memasuki pasar Malaysia dengan keyakinan bahawa mereka tidak melanggar undang-undang tempatan. Sebaliknya, pelabur tempatan juga akan mendapat akses ke tapak-tapak pasaran yang lebih besar di kawasan ASEAN. Namun, jika kerangka hanya sekadar protokol sukar (hardware standards) tanpa kuasa undang-undang (software law), maka Malaysia mungkin hanya menjadi pelabuh bagi kapal-kapal teknologi luar, menumpukan nilai di pelabuh sementara hasil tangannya diminati di atas pelautan.

Aspek Privasi: Antara Jaminan dan Realiti Teknologi

Sementara Fahmi menjanjikan perlindungan data dalam negeri, mekanisme teknologi awan sebenarnya melibatkan pelbagai lapisan enkripsi dan pemprosesan data yang mungkin melibatkan pihak ketiga. Realiti operasi awan awam sering kali melibatkan infrastruktur yang ditempatkan di luar kawalan langsung negara, walaupun data "asli" disimpan dalam negeri. Ini mencipta jurang di mana data boleh dipantau atau diakses di tahap antarabangsa dengan cara yang tidak jelas, menggesa kita bertanya betapa besar sejauh tahap perlindungan yang ditafsirkan Fahmi sebenarnya berfungsi.

Untuk memahami jurang ini, kita perlu membedakan antara "data at rest" (data yang diam) dan "data in transit" (data dalam perjalanan). Fahmi mungkin merujuk pada lokasi fizikal peladang data (data center) yang berada di Malaysia, yang memang mengurangkan risiko pemerolehan fizikal oleh pihak luar secara langsung. Namun, banyak jaringan awan awam menggunakan "multi-tenant architecture", di mana sumber daya komputasi dibahagikan. Meskipun data dienkripsi, kunci enkripsi kadang-kadang didepositkan dengan penyedia jaringan atau di lokasi awan yang tercentralisasi. Jika penyedia awan tersebut berada di luar negeri (seperti AWS, Azure, atau Google Cloud), maka meskipun data "berada di dalam negeri" secara fizikal, proses analisis AI mungkin dijalankan melalui jaringan global.

Ini membawa implikasi privasi yang mendalam. Apakah isi data boleh dibaca oleh sistem AI yang dihosting di luar negeri? Apakah metadata (data tentang data) boleh diekstrak? Dalam era di mana AI generatif boleh mencipta kandungan berdasarkan data latih, "sejarah" data pengguna Malaysia boleh tidak sengaja menjadi bahan latih yang dipindahkan lintas had. Oleh itu, jaminan "data dalam negeri" bukan sekadar lokasi fizikal, tetapi juga kedudukan undang-undang data (data sovereignty law) yang menentukan siapa yang berhak mengakses, bagaimana data boleh digunakan, dan apa jua hukuman bagi pelanggaran. Ketiadaan undang-undang perlindungan data yang kuat di Malaysia akan menjadi penentu sejauh mana janji Fahmi boleh dipegang.

Berapa wang yang dialokasikan untuk projek Awan AI Berdaulat ini?

Adakah penggunaan AI dalam awan ini akan mempengaruhi privasi data peribadi rakyat?

The Daily Star reports Bangladesh Bank's website went offline following a global security alert, confirmed by Executive Director Arief Hossain Khan. The outage has caused immediate unease, paralyzing economic activity by disrupting salary...

The Daily Star reports that Bangladesh Bank's website went offline following a global security alert, confirmed by Executive Director and spokesperson Arief Hossain Khan. The server remains inaccessible as the central bank scrambles to restore services amid rising digital threats. In the immediate aftermath of this disruption, a palpable sense of unease has gripped the nation's financial landscape. For the average citizen and the corporate giant alike, the sight of the blank homepage serves as a stark reminder of how deeply intertwined our daily lives have become with digital infrastructure. When the primary gateway to the banking system closes, it is not just a matter of inconvenience; it is a temporary paralysis of economic activity. The implications are far-reaching, touching everything from the salary disbursement of government employees to the critical Letters of Credit (LCs) that keep the wheels of international trade turning. This incident forces a necessary reckoning with the fragility of a system that many take for granted, exposing the thin line between seamless operation and systemic failure in the face of sophisticated cyber threats.

Let us be clear: this outage is not merely a technical glitch; it is a stark expose of our financial infrastructure's vulnerability in an era where cyber warfare is the new border dispute. While the bank assures that core operations continue, the symbolic paralysis of the nation's financial hub signals deeper rot. The real winner here is not the bank's IT team, but the complacency that has allowed critical systems to run on fumes. The obvious counter-argument suggests that such alerts are routine and the bank has robust backup protocols; however, this view collapses when we consider that a global alert specifically targeting financial institutions has breached the perimeter, proving that our defenses are reactive, not proactive. The stakes are immense: every minute the website is down, interbank transactions stall, investor confidence wavers, and the common citizen feels the tremor in the banking sector's reliability. This incident serves as a litmus test for the resilience of our digital economy, highlighting that in a connected world, an attack on the central bank is an attack on the nation's economic heartbeat.

BB-র সাইবার সুরক্ষা: 'ওয়েবসাইট ডাউন' এর পেছন্দ কি?

The outage coincides with a critical period for Bangladesh's export-oriented sectors. Prothom Alo reports that 18 Bangladeshi textile manufacturers are set to close their stalls at the Spain textile fair today, a direct consequence of disrupted supply chains and logistical hiccups. When the central bank's digital face goes dark, the ripple effect hits the very exporters who contribute billions to the national exchequer. The mechanism is simple: international buyers rely on real-time verification of letters of credit and invoices; if Bangladesh Bank's portal is unreachable, trust erodes. A concrete figure haunts this narrative: the textile sector alone accounts for over 80% of Bangladesh's total export earnings. For these 18 firms, the Spain fair closure is not just a missed opportunity but a tangible revenue loss, especially when competing nations like Vietnam and India keep their digital channels humming. The 'so what' is brutal: every hour of downtime potentially costs the nation millions in foregone foreign currency, turning a technical fault into a trade deficit accelerator. This situation underscores the interconnectedness of financial stability and trade competitiveness, where a glitch in Dhaka can manifest as a lost order in Madrid.

Background context reveals that Bangladesh Bank has historically treated cybersecurity as a compliance checkbox rather than a strategic imperative. Unlike global peers who invest heavily in threat intelligence sharing, our regulatory framework often lags, leaving the banking sector exposed to phishing and ransomware variants that circulate globally. The recent alert, likely stemming from a coordinated international effort to flag vulnerabilities in SWIFT or related messaging systems, exposes this gap. A telling comparison: India's Reserve Bank has a dedicated Cyber Security Strategy Unit that operates 24/7, whereas our BB's response has been largely ad-hoc. The human cost is palpable; small and medium enterprises (SMEs) dependent on daily remittances find their cash flow strangled when the website is offline. The longer-term stakes are clear: if BB cannot guarantee uninterrupted digital access, the migration towards a cashless economy, championed by the government, will stall, pushing businesses back to informal, untracked channels. This regression would not only hinder financial inclusion but also make the economy more susceptible to money laundering and unregulated capital flight.

সাইবার ঋণ: BD-র বাংকিং সিস্টেমের জন্য নতুন মানদণ্ড

The path forward demands a paradigm shift. First, BB must expedite the implementation of the recently proposed Tk 47 crore project for 29 baors (wetlands) by the Fisheries Department, not as an isolated agricultural initiative, but as a testbed for decentralized, resilient data infrastructure. By integrating IoT sensors in baors with BB's monitoring systems, we can create a redundant data flow that survives central server attacks. This innovative approach leverages the natural resilience of distributed ecosystems to bolster financial cybersecurity. Second, the mandatory food business registration starting November 1, as reported by Dhakapost, should be leveraged to build a parallel, secure digital identity for entrepreneurs. This would ensure that even if BB's main website falters, the ecosystem remains partially functional, allowing SMEs to maintain basic operational continuity. The key forecast is that within two years, if BB adopts a multi-layered authentication protocol—moving beyond simple passwords to biometric and hardware token verification—the frequency of successful cyber attacks will drop by at least 40%, according to global cybersecurity trends. The real opportunity lies in transforming this outage from a crisis into a catalyst for a robust, homegrown cyber resilience framework that protects both the institution and the public it serves.

#996. Geopolitical Flashpoints: Middle East Standoff Intensifies as Iran Warns U.S. Navy and Riyadh Intercepts Missile

Tensions across the Middle East escalated sharply as Iran warned the U.S. military against entering the Gulf, while Saudi air defenses intercepted a Houthi ballistic missile targeting Riyadh. Concurrently, President Trump kept global markets on edge regarding a potential U.S. strike on Iran.

An expanding arc of military friction across the Middle East brought multiple regional and global adversaries into direct confrontation on October 25, 2026, as high-stakes posturing translated into kinetic action. The Iranian army announced it shot down a U.S. drone over Bandar Abbas, punctuating a day that also saw a ballistic missile intercepted over the Saudi capital and President Donald Trump refusing to rule out immediate military action against Tehran. These intersecting escalations mark a dangerous new phase in the ongoing nuclear and territorial standoff, testing the limits of deterrence across vital global waterways and energy corridors.

The Bandar Abbas Drone Incident and the Standoff Over the Persian Gulf

The downing of the uncrewed aerial vehicle near the strategic southern Iranian port city of Bandar Abbas represents a major tactical escalation. Iranian military authorities confirmed the engagement early Saturday, asserting that the aircraft had violated national airspace. This kinetic event occurs against a backdrop of rigid ultimatums from Tehran, which has formally warned that the U.S. military is barred from operating inside the Persian Gulf "under any circumstance."

Washington has maintained a formidable naval presence in the region to protect shipping lanes and reassure regional partners, setting up a perilous test of wills. U.S. officials have not yet detailed the operational status of the downed asset, but the incident immediately galvanized maritime security analysts who monitor the Strait of Hormuz. With commercial vessels already navigating under heightened threat conditions, any direct exchange between U.S. and Iranian forces threatens to close or severely restrict one of the world's most critical oil transit arteries.

Riyadh Air Defenses Engage Houthi Ballistic Missile

While maritime flashpoints drew immediate attention in the south, the kingdom of Saudi Arabia faced direct aerial threats from the south. Saudi air defense systems successfully intercepted a Houthi ballistic missile targeting Riyadh, preventing what could have been a catastrophic strike on the national capital. The attack underscores the persistent regional spillover of the broader confrontation involving Iranian-aligned factions.

The interception highlights the ongoing vulnerability of Gulf infrastructure to precision-guided munitions and long-range drones, even as diplomatic channels struggle to secure lasting ceasefires. Riyadh's defense readiness was tested overnight, demonstrating the efficacy of its Patriot and allied missile defense batteries, but also serving as a stark reminder that the conflict remains geographically fluid and capable of reaching major urban centers without warning.

Washington Weighs Potential Military Action as Trump Keeps World on Edge

In the United States, executive rhetoric has amplified global anxiety over an imminent military escalation. When questioned by reporters regarding whether the United States would launch a strike against Iran, President Trump kept the international community on edge with a non-committal "maybe" response. This ambiguity is central to Washington's current strategy of maximum pressure, designed to keep adversary leadership off-balance while military assets are repositioned.

Diplomatic capitals from Europe to Asia are scrambling to interpret the administration's signals, weighing whether the rhetoric heralds a genuine impending campaign or tactical posturing intended to force concessions on the nuclear file. Energy markets have reacted with heightened volatility, pricing in the tangible risk of a sustained military conflict that could disrupt global crude supplies for months.

Regional Shockwaves and the Broader Threat Landscape

The convergence of the Bandar Abbas drone shoot-down, the Riyadh missile interception, and Washington's ambiguous strike threats illustrates a deeply interconnected security crisis. Regional governments in the Gulf find themselves caught between an assertive Iran issuing sweeping territorial bans and an unpredictable U.S. administration weighing kinetic options.

The risk of miscalculation remains acute. Analysts point out that with military units on high alert across multiple front lines, a localized tactical encounter could easily spiral into a wider regional war. Secondary actors and proxy groups continue to probe defensive perimeters, ensuring that no single capital can insulate itself from the broader geopolitical turbulence.

#997. Gulf in Flux: Hormuz Straits, Shifting Alliances and the Shadow of Renewed Conflict

As President Trump warns of a renewed "Epic Fury" scenario in the Persian Gulf, the strategic landscape of the Middle East shifts dramatically with new naval drills, evolving ceasefire dynamics in Yemen, and realigning great-power interests threatening global energy security.

Why the Strait of Hormuz Chokes 20% of the World's Oil

The Persian Gulf’s strategic choke point, the Strait of Hormuz, remains the linchpin of global energy security, through which roughly 20% of the world's oil and nearly all liquefied natural gas exports from Qatar pass daily. As of late October 2026, the waterway is witnessing a marked escalation in military posturing that underscores its fragility. According to recent reports, the United States Navy has increased its presence in the region, conducting freedom of navigation operations aimed at countering perceived threats from Iran. This heightened activity comes amid warnings from Iranian officials that any disruption to their oil exports would trigger a response capable of closing the strait, a scenario that could send global oil prices spiraling. The strait's narrow width—just 21 miles at its narrowest point—means that any military engagement, even a localized one, has the potential to disrupt supplies for the world's largest economies, from Europe to Asia. The current environment is further complicated by the presence of commercial vessels navigating these waters, which must now contend with the risk of accidental clashes or intentional harassment by state and non-state actors alike.

Trump's "Epic Fury" Warning and the Return of Maximum Pressure

President Donald Trump’s recent remarks warning of "another little Epic Fury" have injected a new level of volatility into an already tense region. The "Epic Fury" reference appears to harken back to the maximum pressure campaign of the previous administration, which sought to isolate Iran economically and militarily. Trump’s warning suggests a return to a policy of aggressive deterrence, potentially signaling that the U.S. is prepared to escalate military commitments if Iran continues its uranium enrichment activities or supports proxy operations. This rhetoric is not merely political posturing; it reflects a recalibration of U.S. strategic priorities in the Middle East, where the administration appears willing to accept higher risks of conflict to prevent what it views as Iranian aggression. The warning has been met with cautious analysis from regional experts, who note that while the language is bellicose, the actual policy shifts may be more nuanced, focusing on strengthening regional partners like Saudi Arabia and the UAE while maintaining a delicate balance with Iran to avoid a full-scale war that could destabilize the entire Middle East.

Iran's Drift from the Nuclear Deal and Regional Reactions

Iran’s nuclear program has advanced significantly beyond the limits set by the 2015 Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action (JCPOA), a development that has profound implications for regional and global security. As of the latest reporting, Iran has enriched uranium to levels approaching 60%, a short technical step away from weapons-grade material of 90%. This rapid progression comes as indirect talks between Tehran and world powers have effectively stalled, leaving a diplomatic vacuum that has been filled by military posturing and regional proxy activity. Neighboring Gulf states, particularly Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates, are watching these developments with increasing apprehension. Riyadh has indicated that it may reconsider its own security architecture and potentially pursue indigenous nuclear capabilities if Iran's breakout capacity continues to grow. The international community, led by the European signatories to the JCPOA, has expressed deep concern, urging restraint and a return to dialogue, but the window for diplomatic resolution appears to be narrowing as both sides entrench their positions.

The Houthi Threat and the Yemen Ceasefire’s Fragile State

In Yemen, the Iran-backed Houthi rebels have continued their campaign of cross-border attacks against Saudi territory, even as a UN-brokered truce has nominally held since late 2023. Recent weeks have seen an uptick in drone and missile launches targeting Saudi infrastructure, which Riyadh has intercepted, but the underlying conflict remains unresolved. The Houthis have justified these actions as support for Palestinians in the Gaza Strip, linking the Yemeni conflict to the broader regional fallout from the ongoing Israel-Hamas war. This linkage complicates any efforts to isolate the Yemeni conflict, as it becomes entangled with the wider Palestinian issue and the strategic calculations of Iran. The humanitarian situation in Yemen remains dire, with millions dependent on aid, and the continued militarization of the border threatens to push the country back into full-scale war. Regional actors, including Oman and Qatar, are playing mediating roles, but the prospects for a durable peace remain slim as long as the broader regional rivalry between Iran and the Saudi-led coalition persists.

Great-Power Rivalries and the Redrawing of Alliances

Beyond the immediate actors of Iran and the Gulf Arab states, the current flashpoint is being shaped by the broader great-power rivalry between the United States, China, and Russia. China’s growing economic influence in the region, particularly through the Belt and Road Initiative, has positioned Beijing as a key player in any future energy or security arrangements. Meanwhile, Russia has maintained a strong relationship with Iran, providing military equipment and diplomatic support that bolsters Tehran’s position against the U.S. and its allies. This triangulation of interests means that any escalation in the Gulf is unlikely to remain contained; it could trigger a broader response involving naval assets from multiple powers. The recent joint naval drills between Iran and Russia further signal a deepening of this partnership, potentially altering the balance of power in the Mediterranean and the Gulf. For the U.S., the challenge lies in maintaining its traditional security commitments to Gulf allies while navigating a complex web of diplomatic and military relationships with rival powers who have their own strategic interests in the region.

The Humanitarian and Economic Ripple Effects

The consequences of the current Gulf flashpoint extend far beyond the immediate theater of military operations, touching on global humanitarian and economic systems. A disruption of Hormuz traffic would not only spike energy prices but also exacerbate inflationary pressures in economies already struggling with post-pandemic recovery. Developing nations, which are most vulnerable to energy cost shocks, could face heightened food insecurity as transport costs for goods rise. The psychological impact on seafarers and coastal communities, who live with the constant threat of conflict, adds a human dimension to the geopolitical calculations. International humanitarian organizations have warned that any renewed conflict could disrupt aid flows to already fragile regions, including Yemen and the Horn of Africa. The interconnectedness of these risks means that policymakers must balance the immediate security imperatives of the Gulf with the longer-term goal of global economic stability and humanitarian protection.

What to Watch Next: Scenarios and Flashpoints

Looking ahead, several key scenarios are worth monitoring as the situation evolves. First, the possibility of a deliberate Iranian move to close the Strait of Hormuz in response to renewed sanctions or military action remains the most acute flashpoint, one that could trigger a rapid and severe international response. Second, the trajectory of the U.S.-Iran nuclear talks—currently at a standstill—could either lead to a gradual de-escalation if both sides return to the negotiating table or a dangerous acceleration of enrichment that narrows the path to diplomacy. Third, the internal politics of Gulf states, particularly Saudi Arabia’s succession planning and its security relationship with the U.S., will shape the region's defensive postures. Finally, the role of non-state actors, including the Houthis and other proxy groups, will determine whether the conflict remains a bilateral U.S.-Iran confrontation or expands into a broader regional war. Analysts suggest that the coming months will be critical in determining whether the region moves toward a precarious but manageable status quo or slides into a conflict with unpredictable and far-reaching consequences.

#998. Geopolitical Flashpoints: Tehran's Gulf Warning Shakes Global Oil Markets

With Iran issuing fresh maritime restrictions and the United States deploying additional naval assets to the Persian Gulf, global oil supplies face renewed volatility as diplomatic channels freeze and regional allies brace for escalation. The standoff risks reshaping energy markets and security architectures far…

Why the Strait of Hormuz Chokes 20% of the World's Oil

The Strait of Hormuz remains the single most critical chokepoint in the global energy architecture, through which approximately 20% of worldwide petroleum liquids and 30% of liquefied natural gas shipments pass daily. As of late October 2026, Iran has issued a series of maritime advisories declaring large swathes of the strategic waterway off-limits to commercial traffic, citing "security concerns" following what Tehran describes as provocative U.S. naval maneuvers. The latest directive, announced on 22 October, requires all foreign vessels to obtain prior authorization from Iranian port authorities before traversing the strait’s northern corridor — a move that effectively grants Tehran the power to delay or deny passage at will.

This latest escalation builds on a pattern of reciprocal restrictions that have intensified since the collapse of the Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action (JCPOA) negotiations in early 2025. Iranian officials have repeatedly warned that any attempt to enforce freedom of navigation against their declared zones would be met with "decisive" responses, including the deployment of fast-attack craft and anti-ship missiles. The strategic implications are stark: a sustained closure or significant slowdown of traffic through Hormuz would not only spike global fuel prices but also trigger immediate supply disruptions for major importers in Asia and Europe, many of whom have already been grappling with elevated energy costs throughout 2026.

The United States, which maintains the Fifth Fleet’s headquarters in Bahrain and conducts regular patrols to ensure the free flow of commerce, has responded by accelerating the deployment of additional destroyers and aerial surveillance assets to the region. U.S. Central Command officials have characterized the Iranian advisories as "unacceptable" and a direct threat to international maritime security, promising to "respond appropriately" to any interference with lawful shipping. Meanwhile, regional allies such as Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates have quietly accelerated their own strategic fuel reserves and explored alternative overland and rail corridors, though analysts note these alternatives remain limited by capacity and geopolitical feasibility. The standoff over Hormuz thus serves as both a literal and figurative bottleneck, where the simple act of a tanker passing through has become a barometer of broader U.S.-Iran relations.

Netanyahu’s Warning and the Shadow of Regional War

Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu has entered the escalating fray with a stark warning that Iran may attempt to disrupt upcoming international events, including what he described as a fragile electoral process in the region. In a statement posted on 24 October, Netanyahu declared, “Iran must not test us,” emphasizing that his government would not tolerate any threat to Israeli security interests or the stability of friendly Arab states. This rhetoric aligns with a broader Israeli strategy of holding Iran accountable for proxy activities across Lebanon, Syria, and Yemen, particularly the continued missile and drone capabilities of Lebanese group Hezbollah and Houthi forces in northern Yemen.

Netanyahu’s remarks come at a time when the Israeli military has been conducting intensified operations against Hezbollah infrastructure in southern Lebanon, aiming to degrade the group’s rocket arsenal and rebuild its northern border communities for the return of displaced residents. The Israeli Defense Forces have reported the elimination of several senior commanders and the seizure of weapons caches in recent weeks, steps that Tehran has condemned as “cross-border aggression.” In turn, the Iranian Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) has accelerated its own military exercises, conducting joint drills with Russia in the Caspian Sea and testing new long-range cruise missile variants capable of reaching Israeli territory.

The convergence of these developments raises the prospect of a multi-front confrontation. Should Iran perceive an opening to strike at Israeli or U.S. assets — whether through direct action or via its network of allied militias — the resulting retaliation could rapidly spiral into a broader regional war. Analysts point out that the "gray zone" of cyberattacks, maritime harassment, and proxy engagements is already active, with recent incidents including the reported disabling of an Israeli-owned cargo ship in the Red Sea and Houthi claims of striking a U.S. naval vessel with an unmanned aerial vehicle. The common denominator in these exchanges is Iran’s demonstrated willingness to exploit any perceived weakness in its adversaries’ calculus, making the Netanyahu warning a significant data point in assessing the durability of the current ceasefire arrangements.

Trump’s Strategic Calculus: Deal Versus "Finish Them Off"

Former U.S. President Donald Trump, now the Republican Party’s presumptive nominee for the 2026 presidential election, has inserted himself directly into the escalating crisis with a public declaration that he is "weighing a deal with Iran against action to 'finish them off.'" Speaking at a rally in Ohio on 23 October, Trump framed the choice as one between a diplomatic arrangement that would "make everyone rich" and a more aggressive posture that would "stop Iran’s nuclear ambitions once and for all." His comments have reignited debates within Washington about the feasibility and consequences of a renewed "maximum pressure" campaign, particularly given that the Biden administration has thus far prioritized diplomatic engagement and incremental sanctions relief.

Trump’s rhetoric reflects a segment of the American electorate and foreign-policy establishment that views the Iranian regime as an existential threat to both regional stability and global energy security. Proponents of a harder line argue that only the credible threat of military action — or its execution — can compel Tehran to verifiably roll back its enriched uranium stockpiles, which independent inspectors have confirmed have grown to weapons-grade levels not seen since 2018. Opponents, however, warn that a military strike on Iranian nuclear facilities would likely trigger immediate retaliation against Israel, U.S. bases in the Gulf, and commercial shipping, potentially plunging the Middle East into a conflict far larger than any since the 2003 Iraq invasion.

The timing of Trump’s intervention is particularly consequential, as the United States approaches a pivotal election cycle where foreign-policy credentials will be scrutinized intensely. His stance also puts pressure on the current administration to articulate a clear red line regarding Iran’s nuclear program, especially after the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) reported in September 2026 that Iran has accelerated centrifuge cascades at its Fordow site, moving closer to the 90% enrichment threshold. Whether Trump’s "deal versus finish them off" framing will translate into concrete policy shifts remains uncertain, but his intervention has undoubtedly complicated an already fraught diplomatic landscape, forcing both Tehran and Washington to recalibrate their expectations of what the other side might tolerate.

Humanitarian Fallout and the Civilian Toll

Beyond the strategic posturing and military deployments, the human cost of the deepening Gulf standoff is becoming increasingly visible. In Iran, economic sanctions re-imposed and tightened throughout 2025 and 2026 have contributed to a sharp depreciation of the rial, pushing inflation above 45% and eroding purchasing power for ordinary citizens. The latest round of maritime restrictions has further disrupted trade, with small-scale fishermen and port workers reporting canceled orders and idle docks. Human rights organizations have documented a rise in arbitrary detentions of dual nationals and journalists, many of whom are accused of "spreading propaganda" for merely reporting on the naval tensions.

The civilian impact extends beyond Iran’s borders. In the Gulf Cooperation Council (GCC) states, businesses dependent on stable energy revenues have begun hoarding supplies, while expatriate communities — particularly from South Asia and Africa — face uncertainty as companies delay wage payments amid projected revenue shortfalls. Neighboring Iraq, which relies on both Iranian gas imports and overland trade routes through the strait’s periphery, has seen its own power-generation capacity threatened as fuel shipments slow. The United Nations Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs (OCHA) has warned that any prolonged disruption to Gulf energy flows could exacerbate already precarious food-security situations in import-dependent nations across Africa and the Middle East, where even modest price spikes can trigger social unrest.

On the humanitarian front, international agencies are also monitoring the safety of crew members aboard vessels that find themselves caught in the crossfire of escalating naval postures. The International Maritime Bureau has recorded a 30% increase in reported incidents of "piracy, armed robbery, and suspicious interference" in the Gulf and Strait of Hormuz since the beginning of the fourth quarter of 2026, with many seafarers describing intimidating radio warnings and sudden course changes demanded by Iranian patrol vessels. While no major casualties have been reported to date, the psychological toll on the approximately 1.5 million seafarers who transit the region annually is a growing concern for global labor rights groups. The intertwining of geopolitical brinkmanship and everyday human suffering underscores the urgency of finding a de-escalatory pathway before the costs become truly irreversible.

International Response and the Diplomacy of Restraint

The international community’s response to the latest Gulf escalation has been characterized by a mix of firm condemnation and cautious diplomacy. The European Union, through its High Representative for Foreign Affairs, has reaffirmed support for "the free and safe navigation of international waterways" while simultaneously urging all parties to exercise maximum restraint. EU foreign ministers convened an emergency session on 23 October, resulting in a statement that called for the immediate de-escalation of Iranian maritime advisories and the resumption of structured nuclear talks, though no new sanctions were announced. The EU’s position reflects a broader desire to avoid a energy shock that could derail the bloc’s post-pandemic recovery and climate-transition objectives.

China and Russia, both permanent veto-wielders on the UN Security Council and key economic partners of Iran, have called for a "political solution" rather than military confrontation. Beijing, which imports the majority of its crude oil via routes that pass near the Strait of Hormuz, has issued a discreet diplomatic demarche to Tehran, emphasizing the importance of maintaining stable trade flows for the global economy. Moscow, for its part, has continued its own naval cooperation with Iran, conducting the aforementioned Caspian Sea drills and selling advanced air-defense systems that Iran has integrated into its coastal protection grid. Both capitals have warned that any use of force would have "unpredictable consequences," a phrase that many interpret as a veiled caution against Washington entertaining strike options.

At the multilateral level, the IAEA has maintained its monitoring presence in Iran, with inspectors reporting regularly to Vienna on the country’s enrichment activities. The agency’s most recent quarterly report, released in early October 2026, noted that Iran’s stockpile of enriched uranium has grown to over 400 kilograms, with a significant portion enriched to 60% — a short technical step from weapons-grade material. IAEA Director General Rafael Grossi has called for "greater transparency" and "predictable access" to all declared and undeclared sites, warning that the window for a diplomatic resolution is narrowing. Meanwhile, back-channel contacts between Washington and Tehran, mediated by Oman and Qatar, have reportedly continued, though officials on both sides have described the exchanges as "exploratory" rather than indicative of a breakthrough. The overarching picture is one of a system under strain, where every diplomatic move is mirrored by a corresponding military posturing, and the prospect of a miscalculation that triggers a wider conflict remains a persistent, sobering reality.

What to Watch Next: Scenarios and Flashpoints

Several converging developments merit close attention in the coming weeks. First, the extent to which Iran chooses to enforce its new maritime advisory — whether it results in sporadic delays, selective inspections, or a full de facto closure of the strait’s northern corridor — will be a primary indicator of intent. Any sustained disruption would likely prompt a rapid U.S. naval counter-deployment and could force regional allies to activate emergency oil reserves, sending shockwaves through already volatile markets. Second, the response of the Israeli government to any perceived Iranian provocation, particularly through its proxy networks in Lebanon and Yemen, will shape the risk of a multi-front war. Third, the outcome of the U.S. presidential election, should it occur before a diplomatic breakthrough, could fundamentally alter the calculus: a Trump administration might pursue a more aggressive "maximum pressure" strategy, while a Harris or other Democratic successor could double down on diplomatic engagement and sanctions relief.

A further flashpoint to watch is the potential for accidental escalation through maritime or aerial incidents. The dense concentration of naval assets, commercial shipping, and air-defense systems in the Gulf creates a "hair-trigger" environment where a misread radar return or an unintended missile launch could spiral out of control. Analysts also point to the role of non-state actors, particularly the Houthis in Yemen, who have demonstrated a willingness to target commercial and military vessels as a means of projecting power and extracting concessions. The interplay between these state and non-state variables means that the situation remains highly dynamic, and the next major development — whether a diplomatic opening, a military strike, or an accidental collision at sea — could arrive with little warning. For now, the international community’s best hope lies in the continuation of back-channel diplomacy and the establishment of clear, enforceable de-confliction mechanisms to prevent the Gulf from becoming a theater of open war.

#1000. Nỗi đau 'đầu gấu' Hà Nội: Nợ xã hội đen xâm nhập khu kinh doanh lớn

Khoảng thời gian vừa qua, Hà Nội chứng kiến sự hội tụ của hai dòng biến động lớn: việc xử phạt hành chính hàng hóa giả mạo tại xã Phú Nghĩa và việc mở rộng truy tố hình sự về nợ xã hội đen. Sự kết hợp giữa hai yếu tố này đã tạo nên một "cuộc đua...

Khoảng thời gian vừa qua, Hà Nội đang chứng kiến sự hội tụ của hai dòng biến động lớn, tạo nên một bức tranh tổng thể về những thách thức cấp bách đối mặt với hệ thống kinh doanh địa phương. Không chỉ dừng lại ở việc xử phạt hành chính với những hộ kinh doanh vi phạm, thành phố đang đối mặt với một tình trạng truy tố hình sự ngày càng mở rộng về các khoản nợ "xã hội đen". Sự kết hợp giữa hai yếu tố này không tạo ra những vấn đề độc lập mà đã hình thành nên một "cuộc đua quyền lực" khổng lồ, để lại những tác động sâu sắc đến cơ sở hạ tầng kinh doanh nhỏ lẻ. Dưới đây là phân tích sâu hơn về cơ chế hình thành, bối cảnh địa phương và những hệ quả không thể bỏ qua của hai dòng sự kiện này.

Phát triển bối cảnh vụ xử phạt hàng hóa giả mạo tại xã Phú Nghĩa

Vụ việc tại xã Phú Nghĩa đánh dấu một bước tiến quan trọng trong công tác kiểm soát thị trường nội địa. Theo thông tin từ các nguồn báo chí, khoảng 30 hộ kinh doanh tại khu vực này đã bị cơ quan chức quyền đưa ra quyết định xử phạt hành chính do vi phạm về việc bán hàng giả mạo nhãn hiệu Adidas. Tuy nhiên, sâu xa hơn chỉ việc phạt tiền, những hộ kinh doanh này cũng đối mặt với nguy cơ bị tịch thu hàng hóa và thiệt hại lớn về danh tiếng thương hiệu.

Thực tế kinh tế địa phương cho thấy, Hà Nội là một thị trường tiêu thụ cực kỳ lớn và đa dạng, trong đó các sản phẩm thương hiệu có giá trị cao luôn có nhu cầu tiêu thụ mạnh mẽ. Tuy nhiên, sự xuất hiện của hàng hóa giả mạo không chỉ gây thiệt hại về kinh tế cho các bên quyền lợi thương hiệu nhưng cũng làm nhầm lẫn cho người tiêu dùng về chất lượng sản phẩm. Việc xử phạt những hộ kinh doanh này thể hiện quyết tâm của chính quyền địa phương trong việc bảo vệ quyền lợi người tiêu dùng và duy trì môi trường kinh doanh lành mạnh, minh bạch. Đối với những hộ kinh doanh nhỏ lẻ, con số phạt có thể lên tới hàng chục triệu đồng là một gánh nặng không nhỏ, có thể ảnh hưởng đến khả năng lưu thông vốn và khả năng duy trì hoạt động trong ngắn hạn. Việc này cũng gửi ra một tín hiệu rõ ràng: không gian kinh doanh tại Hà Nội sẽ ngày càng khắt khe hơn về nguồn gốc sản phẩm và thương hiệu.

Sự phồn hoa và nguy cơ của dòng vốn vay không chính thống

Song song với cuộc crackdown hàng hóa, vấn đề nợ xã hội đen đang héo lên như một mối đe dọa tiềm ẩn nhưng cực kỳ nguy hiểm. Theo các báo cáo từ VietNamNet và Dân trí, cơ quan chức quyền đang mở rộng truy cứu trách nhiệm hình sự về các cá nhân liên quan đến các khoản nợ lồng ghép heroin và các hoạt động vay mượn không chính thống. Khác với biện pháp xử phạt hành chính, truy tố hình sự mang lại những hậu quả nặng nề hơn như án tù, hạn chế quyền libertarian và thậm chí là án hình sự.

Cơ chế hoạt động của các nhóm nợ đen thường cực kỳ độc hại. Họ khai thác tâm lý cần vốn của những hộ kinh doanh nhỏ lẻ, cung cấp vốn vay với lãi suất "thịnh thoảng" nhưng thực chất cực kỳ cao, đôi khi có thể lên tới 10-20% mỗi tháng. Khác với lãi suất ngân hàng thông thường chỉ khoảng 7-8% mỗi năm, sự chênh lệch này tạo ra áp lực tài chính không thể chịu đựng được. Nhiều nạn nhân không chỉ mất tài sản mà còn phải đối mặt với áp lực thể chất và tinh thần, thậm chí là đe dọa tính mạng để đảm bảo trả nợ.

Thực tế địa phương cho thấy mối liên hệ chặt chẽ giữa dòng vốn vay không chính thống và hệ thống hộ kinh doanh. Nhiều hộ kinh doanh nhỏ tại Hà Nội, đặc biệt là những doanh nghiệp lẻ bán đồ nội thất, đồ gia dụng hoặc thực phẩm, thường xuyên cần vốn luân chuyển để duy trì lưu thông vốn hoạt động. Khi cơ chế vay mượn không chính thống xâm nhập vào khu vực này, người kinh doanh dễ dàng trở thành mục tiêu dễ bị bắt nạt của các nhóm tội phạm. Theo số liệu từ các báo cáo về tình hình tài chính hộ kinh doanh, tỷ lệ vay mượn không chính thống tại các khu vực như Hoàn Kiếm, Tây Hồ hay các khu phố cũ Hà Nội có thể lên tới 20-30% so với tổng vốn lưu chuyển. Con số này không chỉ là một vấn đề tài chính cá nhân mà đã trở thành rủi ro hệ thống có thể gây sập đổ toàn bộ mô hình kinh doanh của một hộ nếu không có sự can thiệp kịp thời.

Cơ chế hình thành "cuộc đua quyền lực" và tác động lên hộ kinh doanh

Sự khác biệt lớn nhất giữa hai vụ việc này nằm ở đối tượng chịu ảnh hưởng và mức độ can thiệp, nhưng hậu quả của chúng lại trùng chồng lên nhau ở tầng cơ sở nhất. Việc xử phạt hộ kinh doanh bán hàng giả mạo là biện pháp phòng ngừa, nhằm bảo vệ quyền lợi người tiêu dùng và gìn giữ thương hiệu, trong khi truy tố hình sự về nợ xã hội đen là biện pháp xử lý hậu quả, nhằm phạt bỏ những nhóm tội phạm sử dụng quyền lực để bắt nạt.

Tuy nhiên, hậu quả của hai biện pháp này lại trùng chồng lên nhau ở tầng cơ sở: những hộ kinh doanh nhỏ lẻ đang là người liên kết giữa hai thế giới này. Họ vừa phải đối mặt với kiểm soát chặt chẽ của cơ quan quản lý thị trường về hàng hóa, vừa phải担心 (theo yêu cầu ngữ cảnh, dùng từ "lo ngại") bị truy cứu trách nhiệm hình sự do nợ viền. Điều này tạo ra một áp lực kép khiến nhiều doanh nghiệp phải cân nhắc dừng hoạt động hoặc chuyển đổi mô hình kinh doanh để tránh rủi ro.

Cụ thể, một hộ kinh doanh bán đồ nội thất nhỏ có thể nhận vốn vay từ một người mượn không chính thống để mua nguyên liệu hoặc đầu tư trang thiết bị. Nếu không thể trả nợ vào thời hạn do lãi suất quá cao hoặc các biện pháp bắt nạt, hộ kinh doanh đó có thể sụp đổ. Ngay cả khi họ không tham gia vào việc bán hàng giả mạo, họ vẫn có thể trở thành mục tiêu của cơ quan kiểm soát nếu hoạt động của mình không minh bạch hoặc vi phạm các quy định về nguồn gốc hàng hóa. Ngược lại, nếu họ bán hàng giả mạo để tăng thu nhập để trả nợ, họ sẽ bị phạt hành chính và có thể bị truy tố nếu hành vi liên quan đến tội phạm xuyên giới.

Dự báo ngắn hạn cho thấy nếu không có các chính sách hỗ trợ tài chính và pháp lý rõ ràng, số lượng hộ kinh doanh bị ảnh hưởng sẽ tiếp tục tăng trưởng. Cụ thể, các chuyên gia dự đoán rằng trong quý 4/2026 và đầu năm 2026, Hà Nội có thể sẽ tiếp tục có khoảng 10-15 vụ xử phạt mới về hàng giả mạo cùng với các vụ truy tố hình sự liên quan đến nợ viền tại các khu kinh doanh dịch chuyển.

Số liệu này nếu không được xử lý kịp thời sẽ khiến sự tự tin của người tiêu dùng và nhà đầu tư nhỏ lẻ bị rung lắc, ảnh hưởng trực tiếp đến mục tiêu tăng trưởng kinh doanh địa phương của Thủ đô. Người tiêu dùng có thể càng càng chút chậm lựa chọn sản phẩm tại các cửa hàng nhỏ lẻ do sợ mua phải hàng giả hoặc sợ rủi ro pháp lý do nợ viền. Cùng thời điểm, nhà đầu tư có thể ngần ngại đầu tư vốn vào các dự án kinh doanh nhỏ do lo ngại về môi trường pháp lý ngày càng phức tạp.

Kết luận: Kéo dài nỗi đau và con đường khắc phục

Tóm lại, Hà Nội đang đứng trước một giai đoạn chuyển dịch khó khăn nơi hai dòng biến động – việc kiểm soát hàng hóa giả mạo và truy tố nợ xã hội đen – đang tác động song song đến hệ thống kinh doanh nhỏ lẻ. "Cuộc đua quyền lực" này không chỉ tạo ra áp lực pháp lý mà còn ảnh hưởng đến tinh thần kinh doanh, dòng vốn và niềm tin của cộng đồng.

Để thoát khỏi vòng xoáy này, cần có sự kết hợp giữa việc tăng cường giáo dục pháp lý cho người kinh doanh và người tiêu dùng, đồng thời có các chính sách hỗ trợ tài chính dự phòng cho những hộ kinh doanh đang gặp khó khăn do nợ viền. Chỉ có những biện pháp toàn diện và đồng bộ hóa mới có thể đảm bảo rằng hệ thống kinh doanh địa phương của Hà Nội có thể phát triển bền vững, bảo vệ được quyền lợi của mọi bên liên quan mà không để bất kỳ hộ kinh doanh nào trở thành "người thua thiệt" trong cuộc đua quyền lực này.

Khách hàng đã mua hàng giả mạo tại khu Phú Nghĩa cần làm gì khi được phạt?

Người kinh doanh nhỏ có bị truy tố hình sự vì nợ viền không?
